# Constituent Assembly of India (Legislative) Debates, 28 February 1949 — Budget for 1949-50 (Dr. John Matthai) (FULL VOLUME TEXT)

**Source header**
- Corpus: Constituent Assembly of India (Legislative) Debates, Fourth Session, Volume II, 1949. 1947–1950 = Constituent Assembly of India (Legislative), NOT the Lok Sabha.
- Date of sitting: 28 February 1949 (Budget for 1949-50 presented)
- Official publisher: Lok Sabha Secretariat / Parliament of India; Parliament Digital Library (eParlib)
  handle `https://eparlib.sansad.in/handle/123456789/760468`, bitstream `https://eparlib.sansad.in/bitstream/123456789/760468/1/cald_04_28-02-1949.pdf`
- **Route actually used — READ THIS (intermediary):** `eparlib.sansad.in` is unreachable from this
  session (the egress gateway answers 403 to CONNECT for `eparlib.nic.in` / `archive.org` alike, and
  all direct `curl` is blocked). The text below was obtained from the **Internet Archive mirror** of
  the official eParlib scan — IA item `archive.org/details/eparlib.nic.in.760468`, OCR derivative fetched at
  `https://archive.org/cors/eparlib.nic.in.760468/cald_04_28-02-1949_djvu.txt` — retrieved via the Firecrawl MCP `firecrawl_scrape` tool.
  This is therefore an **Internet Archive Tesseract-OCR rendering of the official printed volume**,
  NOT a direct read of the Sansad website and NOT a read of the printed page. OCR errors are
  frequent and visible (digits are especially unreliable — leading "1"s are routinely dropped, so
  "1949" often prints as "949" and "£1,537 million" as "£,537 millions"; running-head years are
  sometimes wrong). Substantive prose is legible. **Any figure quoted from this file must be
  re-checked against the printed volume before publication.**
- Retrieved: 2026-08-17
- Keyword counts across this whole file (case-insensitive, whole file including index/contents):
  sterling 11 · Britain 0 · British 8 ·
  London 0 · balances 7 · agreement 16

- **Why this document matters:** paragraphs 7–11 of the Finance Minister's Budget speech, under
  the headings 'Balance of Payments' and following, are an explicit official statement that the
  United Kingdom unilaterally limited the convertibility of India's own sterling from January 1948,
  in amounts that 'bear no relation whatever to our needs', and that India's whole import policy is
  bounded by 'the amount by which it has been agreed with the United Kingdom Government that
  India's sterling balances should be drawn upon'. The budget speech begins around character
  250,000 of this file; the sterling passages are at roughly characters 259,500–272,000.
- **OCR warning specific to this file:** every figure in it has lost leading digits. '£10 million'
  prints as '& 0 million'; '£25 million' as '£ 5 mitlion'; 'Rs. 1,733 crores' as 'Rs. ,733 crores';
  '1948' as '948'. The figures below have been reconstructed in the finding file from context and
  MUST be verified against the printed volume.

ENQvIRY INTO CosT of PRopvorioN OF CoaL 

*75i. Shri R, L. Maliviya: (a) Wiil the Henourable Minister of Industry 
and Supply be pleased to state whether for purp ses of implemertating the 
awards of the Conciliation Board in the Coal mines in Bengal and Bihar and that 
ef the Fact Finding Committee in the Central Provinces and enhancing the 
prico of coal, any enquiry was made by Government into the cost of produc- 
tion of coal? ‘ 

(b) If so, who made tiie enquiry and what wus the cost of production of 
coal per ton in different collieries in Bengal, Bihar and the Centra! T’rov.nces? 

(०) What was the procedure adopted for such enquiry? 


(d) If not, how was the rate of incrcase in Bihar and Bengal at is. 8/8 per 
ton and Rs. 3 per ton in the Central Provinces arrived at? 


The Hocourabls Dr. Syama Prasad Mookarjee: (a) Yes, Sir. 

(0०) The enquiry was made by Government through experienced officera with 
knowledge of working of coul mines and Cost Accounting. In the case of Bengal! 
Bibar coalficlds, the advice of the Commodities Prices Board was also taken, 
The average production cost per ton from the representative collieries was 
estimated २० range from Rs. @0 to Rs. 4-2 excluding colliery consumption 
and free issues to labour in the case of Bengal/Bihar and from Re. 33-8-9 to 
Re. 5-8 in the case of C.P. and Berar. . 


(०) The production cost per ton was arrived at ou the ७७२४ of the cost sheate 
furnished by the.collieries snd after investigation into the accounts of the 
collieries which were considered representative in character. 


(9) ‘The quantum of increase granted in the selting prices vf coal was 
artived at on the basis of the production cost per ton after taking into account 
among other factors tbe estimated increase consequent. ou the grant of increased 
‘wages and other concessions to colliery, !abeur plus a reasonable margin for 
profit, The inorease granted in the selling prices of C.P. and Berar ranged from 
Re, 3-8 to Rs. 3-7 per ton instead of Rs. 3 per ton as mentiened by the 
honourable member. ..

from each and every coal geld? e 
. The Hanowrable Dr. Syama Prasad Mookarjee: I have just explained that 
we selected some representative collieries.

SHORT NOTICE QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS 


THERATENED Rattway Srame snp ACTION aGainst CommMUNBIS 


Seri Deghbandbu Gupta: Will the Honoureble the Prime Minister be 
Pleased to atate: 


(a) the number of commumiste who have been arreated during the last three 
daya in different ppovinces of the country as a precautionary measure against 
the threatened railway strike; and 


(b) what other action Government propose to take fo evaure that no obetruc- 
tion is placed in the way of Railway staf? and workers, who are opposed to the 
strike by the Commuuiste? 


The Hanonrahle Shri Jawaharlal Webkru: Sir, witl your permission, I pro- 
pose to reply to botb parte of this question in the form of a statement, which 
i think wii) interest the Hovae. 


2. The total uumber of persous arrested during the lsat ten days, in con- 
aection wiih the proposed railway strike. is 870. This figure is accurate up 
till yesterday. 


8. The policy ०६ Government has been not io interfere. in any way, with 
the functioning of trade wnions and other labour organisations. Goverment 
are of opinion that the formation and the legitimate functiouing of trade unions 
ahoold be , 89 thst labour may play ita proper part in industry and 
in the promotion of its own iuterests. Government [५४४९ uttempted to give the 
widest latitude to al] 68804] propagenda of any polioy or doctrine, even though 
they were opposed to that doctrine. Government hava refrained from taking 
any action egainst the Communic: Part.v of Indiz as such, in spite of 8 virulent 
propaganda and incitement to violence which the Communist Party members 
have indulged in. Where violence has been sctugily attempted or encouraged, 
Government have teken action. Suoh action has been limited to individuals who 
were connsoted with this violence or encouragement of violance. 


4. The Communist Party of India hes, during the past year, adopted an 
attitude not only of open hostility ४० Government but one,whioh can be described 
us bordering on ojen revolt. ‘This policy has been given effect tu inteusively 
in certain limited areas of india and hae resulted iv violence including murders, 
urson, and footing us well as acte of sabotage. The House is well aware of 
the communiet revolts that have takeu pluce in countries bordering on India. It 
wos presumably in furtherance of the same policy that ebrampts were meade 
in Iudia to bagged de people to active revelt. Fortunately, these attempts failed 
beeanse of the of popular opinion agsiust them and the action taken 
by. Goveenmant. Nevertheleea @ great deal of misery 800 damage was caused 
by them in exrtain perts of India 


3440 CONSTITUENY ABBKMBLY OF LPIA (LILGIsLAlive) (20TH 0४७, 3४4४ 


5, The House is fully aware that for the last several moutba we have bean 
Grestly pteoceupied with meeting the serious esonomio aitustion which hae 
been cauemg distress to Milions or our people. 4t waa implicit in our programme 
that there should be rapid movement of commodities throughout the oountzy. 


6. Towards the end of November, the Working Committee of the All India 
silwaymen'’s Federation met in Nagpur and decided on taking 8 strikes ballot 
among the members efbiiated to it. At this time we bed additional canfirmation 
of intormation which hed bean caming to us from me to time, that certain 

‘uniat elements working in the railway unions were bent on exploiting the 
proposed atrike for political purposes and, in particular, on using violence and 
sabotage to gain their 60908. 


7. Negotiations then took pilase between my culleague, the Transport Minister 
and Sbri Jayaprakssh Narayan, the president of the All India Reilwaymen'sa 
Federation, with a view to devising Means for averting the atr.ke. It was clear 
to Government that 8. strike in tbe railways would, perweularly et the present 
juncture, be an unmitigated disaster to the country. Apart from the fact that 
dieloc#tion of transportation would nullity the steps thut Governmeut have been 
taking to meet the serioua eoanamic situstion facing the country, a famine was 
developing in Gujarat and Kutch and a strike would be disastrous to millions 
of human beings and cattle in these areas. ‘T'hese discussions were conducted 
in a Gandy atmosphere and certam arrangemeuts were agread to. As a result, 
the Railwaymen's Kederstion decided not to proceed with the atrike, 


8. In spite of this decision of the Railwaymeu's Vederstion, certain com- 
munist elemente in the Federation proceeded with their programme for a atrike. 
Reporte continue to reach Governmant, through relisbla eources, thst a wide- 
spread programme of sabotage hed been planned and thet it was decided to 
enforce the sirike by means of violence. Indeed, many sich instances of 
eabotage have already occurred. Mecantly thers were unfortunate olachas 
hetwecu some students and otbers eng the police in Cuioutta, and during these 
clashes, hand_grenades and bombs were used against the police and publio 
property, suoh as buses and tramoars were destroyed. We have evidence that 
the ७४006 egencies which provided theee weapons to the students are exploiting 
tbem for similar pucpoaes dunag the railway atrike. 


9. The Communist Party of India has recently copcevtzated on the issue 
of a general atrike on the cailwaye as well ae in other essential services of 
paramount imporlance to the community. it bas looked upon these strikes 
not from the trade union or econamic point of view, meant to better the lot 
of we workers, but 88 a weapon designed to creste a chaotic state in the county 
which, it is thought, would help the rea to gain ita other objectives, whatever 
they migbt be. It ig deliberately ing %© create famine conditions by 
paralyeing the railway system, co that foodstuffs sbould net 96 
the object being to create a genera! baskground of chace, a breakdown of the 
ednunistation and mase uprisings. A large number of peamiment commuuisd 
party members have gone uuderground and Govemment have a mass of 
evidence in their possession to indicato that organised attempta are being made 
to couduct campaigns of sabotege, more eepecially on the railway aystem.. The 
pemusnent way was to be damaged, locomotives interfered with, and general 
ssbotage of vital installations, telephones, telegraphs and power stations was 
aimed at. Honourable Members will reamamber the destruction of the 
(0०३०० ४४७ Telephone Exchange some time ago. 


30, Fortunstely, the great majority of railwaymen and othar workers have 
dealarad themselves opposed to the general strike aud to suoh methods. Tho 
Cammunist Party of India, however, appears to be bent on floutmg the 


STARRED QUESTIONS AND 4NSWERS wii 


opinion of this majority of workers and has pursued a technique of terzorising 
those who do not agree with its policy. While interfermg with the freedom 
* of action of otherg,. it demands ful! freedom for itself te carry on its owk 
antisocial and disruptive activities. If any action is taken by Government 
te check these activities, protests are raised ou the ground of civil liberties 
being interfered with. As a part of this technique, organisations for the 
ostensible object of protecting civil liberties are started, their real object 
being to encourage these anti-social activities. Government are ansiowe that 
the civii liberties of the people showld be fully maintained. But it is not 
Government’s conception of civil liberty to permit methods of coercion and 
terrorism to be practised against the geuernl community. It is the para- 
mount duty of Government to give security to the people and to prevent the 
normal life of the community from being interfered with by such methods 
of violence. No Government and no social life would be possible, if these 
methods were tolcrated. Hence Government have been compelled to take 
all necessary measures to meet this situation. If any strike takes place on 
the railways by a fraction of the railwaymen or elsewhere, they are deter- 
mined to deal with~it firmly. Such a strike at this juncture, it must always 
ic remembered, has nothing to do with economic questions or the norn} 
activities of trade unions and the like. Government have already shown 
their earnest desire ५० meet all legitimate demands of their employees and 
other workers. They will always be prepared to consider any difSculties 
that their employees have to face and to remove them as far 98 possible. 
They are determined to maintain the wellestablisbed rights of labour. But 
they cannot submit to threata of violence and incitement to active revolt 
from any quarter. 


li. In furtherance of this policy, Government have arrested = number oi 
members of the Communist Party of India and have taken such other pre- 
cautionary measures as they deem necessary. They have advised provincial 
Governments to do likewise so as to ensure that vite] installations are 
protected against sabotage. They have no doubt that in doing 60 they have 
the full support of the country and of this House, which is wedded to demo. 
cratic procedure and is entirely opposed to methods of violence. 


2. The nature of the activities and abjectives of certain groups ia, the 
counéty can be judged by the events which took place st Dum Dum and 
other places in Calcutta two days ago. A meeting was held in the maidan 
vader the auspices of the Bengal Provincial Trade Union Congress, which is 
a body dominated by the Communist Party of India. 8 peckers urged the 
seizure of power by violent means. A ४६७१७०६ of the Bengal Provincial 
Students Federation, which is ulso dominated by the Communist Party of 
Indie, stated that a saboteur gang of students was ready for action. Maree 
groups had gone out previously on taxies. motorcelx and buses and did con- 
siderable damage. Fourteen of these terrorists were captured on Saturday 
night and a quantity of hand grenades, sten guos and bombs were recovered 
from them. The arreated men belong to what is known as the Revolutionary 
Communist Party of India which broke away from the Communist Party of 
India but collaborates with if on occasions. This gronop of R.C.P.I. men 
wes responsible for the incident at the water works at Talla a few months 
ago, when an attempt was made to blow up the water works machinery. 


3. This recent incident in Calcutta gives some indication of the kind of 
ation that is intended by those people who are working for genera} strikes. 
E have just heard of these cases of aftempte to derail trains during the jast 
two days. It 8 against this kind of action that every precaution has to he 
adaken. Government are sstiefed that the vast number of raitway employees 
gad other workers in the countzy do not want sucb general strike and are 


ili2 CONSTITURNT ASSZMBLY OF INDIA (LEGISLATIVE)  J28re Fer. 949 


opposed fe such methods. It. is Governmeut’s duty uot only to protect the 
general community but also these large numbers of workers. In any aotton 
that Government may take, they will always remember that the workmg 
Glasses are the backbone of the country and that their general intereste must 
not suffer. May I add an expression of the Government's regtet at the 
killing by these tetrorists of a number of persons in Calcutta during the last 
two or thee days. ‘These include a number of police officers and men and 
officers ef Messrs. Jessop Limited and of the B.O.A.C. 


Shri Deshbandhu @upte: In view of this evidence whicb the Government 
have of the Communist Party corroborating with the terrorists, have Gov- 
ernment considered the advisability of declaring it an unlawful association? 


The Honourable Shri Jawaharlai Nehru: The Government are always 
reluctant to declare eny organizatio; as such unlawful. Government will 
even allow, as the House knows, the centinued publication of periodicals, 
which, whether they are Communist or whetber they ule dtherwise, are often 
indulging in tbe rnost virulent. propagauda. No doubt what the honourable 
meniber has suggested is being and will be considered, but Government 
always prefer not to deal with an organisation 82 such but rather with the 
individuals who are in it. 


Shr, Desahbandhu Gupte: May I know, Sir, whether it is a fact that a 
large number of Congressmen, or persons believed to be Congressmen have: 
been killed by Communists in Hyderabad and roundabout areas? If so, what 
ia their approximate number? 


The Honourable Shri Jawaharlai Nehru: When the honourable member 
mentions Congressmen in Hyderabad presumably he means members of the 
State Congress of Hyderabad. I am unable to supply any exact numbers, 
but from the general information we have reoeived, over two hundred 
members of the State Congress have been individually murdered in certain 
areas of Hyderabad. 


Shri Deshbandhu Gupta: Have Government taken ariy action againat the 
publication of the People’s Age in Bombay? Is it a fact that the Newspaper 
Advisory Committee for the province bave advised action against this psper? 

-The Honowable Shri Jawaharlal Nehen: Yes, I believe that the Press 
‘Advisory Cozmiittee of the Bombay Province haa advised the Provincial Gor- 
ernnient #0 tske action against the Pespte’s Age. 

Soti EH. V, Mamath: Je there avy evidence to show that some Indizo 
communists, not content with incitement te violence and revolt qgainst their 
own Government here, gre engaged in fighting And subverting a good neighbour 
Government mean the Burma Government | 

The Honcarahis Shri Jawsharlai Nebru: There may be individuals acting 
in thie way and probably there are. 

Shyi Aram Obandta Quke: Ic the Communlet Party nob bamned in eny 
province in Indie? 0 

Fhe Honontable Shri Jawaharis? Nehro: Yes, in Wesb Benge. 


Mr. B. Poker; May I- know whether. ibe Quéermment bab any information 
as to. the number of Communists in indies? If so, which is the province ip 


which they are the largest in number and bow many of them -beve gode 
amdergrownd? : ; 


The Honourable Skyi Jawxhaifal Mehru: I am afraid I could not give any 


प्र aumbers in soe the, number of Canmuss} 29७09 mambers or 
aot femelle, of ४७५३ whp have. gone : underground: Probably. the 
numbers 2 ¥ involved are ust very big... | is ४ 


STARRED QUESTIONS AND ANSWERG . 833. 


: Shri Minir I.aJ Ohattopafhyay: Is it a fact thet the leader of tbe revolu- 
tionary Communist Party of India was arzeéted by Government under tbe 
Public Safety Act and that he was released six months back? ° # 

The Hououtable Shri Jawaharlal Mehra: I do not quite know except that 
the revolutionary Communist Party of India, whieh was a faotion whiob 
separated from the Conamunist Party, iteelf later split up mito two! 

Sart Mahavir Tyagi: Do you believe it? 

My. Chairman: Order, order. 

Shri MDbir Lal Chattopadhyay: You eay thet the rovolutionary; Communis+ 
Party of India has split up into two parts now? 

{The Honourable Shri Jawaharlal Nehru: I do x0t know who is the leader 
of which group and what happened to him. 

Pandit Lakshmi Kanta Majtra: May I enquire whetber the Government 
is taking any steps te check the infilteation ef Communists from Melaya,and 
Burma. who are :oviing as refugees to this country? 

The Honourable Shri Jawahkatial Nehru: I am not aware of any serious 
danger of that type. Naturally tho Government tries to keep wide awake to 
all such possibilities, But so far there has been no cause for slorim for this 
particular type of danger. i 

Pandit Iakshmi Kanta Maitra: I was enquiring whether the Government 
was aware that those refugees from Burma and Malaya who are coming to 
India are really Communists end the¥ are establishing a link with the Cem- 
munists in this country. Have Government kept a watchful eye on that? 


The Hanotrabie Shri Jawaharla] Nekru: I am ::ot aware of any partioaler 
case of that type. [% is a possibility of course! A 

Strl V. O. Keeava Bao: May I know what steps Government are. taking 
fer eliminating Communists who are honemplnyees? 

The Honourable Shtl Jawaharlal Nehru: I do nét understand this question. 
Eliminating from where? 

Shri V. 0, Kessva Rao: What steps are Government taking to eliminate 
Communiste from the Railwaymen’s Federation who are not employees .of 
the Railways? 

The Hononradle Shri Jawaharial Nehru: | still do uot understand. There 
may be a policy of encouraging or rather discouraging non-workers from being 
office-beazers of the Union, There may be possibly that polioy. But it is 
undesiable to apply political labels to individuals of that type. 

Shri Raj Bebsdor: Have Government ascertained the souree or ageney 
through which the Commnuniste are obtaining theix arms and azsnunition? 

The Honouradle Shri Jawaharlat Mehrn: There muet be eome information 
in possession of Government. Since the last war, there have been many 
ways of getting arms and ammunition, :nore especially in eerteim frontier 
areas—Assam, Bengal. etc., and I bave no donbt that many of these Com- 
munists have been able to get these arms from those parscular sources...

Mr. Wasiraddin Ahmed: Will the Honourable Minister of Law be pleased 
to state: ; 

(a) whether the Departmental Committee set up by the Ministry of Law 
to revise the Hindu Code Bill, referred to the Select Committee on the Gib 
April, 048, was instructed not to make aby substentia? changes in the body « 
० f the original Bill; 


३8४१4 CONSTITUENT ASSRMBLY 07 INDIA (LEGISLATIVE) [26TH Fes. 949 


* (b) what the terms of instructions to the Departmental Committee is this 
reapect were, and 


(c) who the members of the Departmental Committee were? 


The Honoutabie Dr. B. R. Ambedkar: (a) and (b). No such instructions 
were issued to the departmental committee. The first revised draft of the 
Bill was prepared by myself with the subject matter rearranged in a conse- 
cutive and logical form. 

(०) The departmental committee consisfed of .Messrs K. Y. Bhandarkar, 
D. H. Nanavati and 0. R. Rajagopal of the Ministry of Law. Mr. 8. V. 
Gupte an advocate of Bombay was also invited to work on the, Committee. 
I. inyrelt acted us Chairmun of the Committee. 

Mr. Nariraddtn Ahmad: Is the Honourable Minister aware of any sub- 
stantie! changes made in the departmental draft? 

The Honourable Dr. B. ER. Ambedkar: I suppose my honourable friend 
will next: a8k us to what changes have been made. 

Dr. P. 8. Deshmukh: At what stage was this Bill converted into an official 
Bil? 

The Honoursbie Dr. 8. R. Ambedkar: It was always an official Bill when 
it was introduced. 

Dr. P. 8. Desbmukh: Is there any cabinet decision on record? 


The Honourable Dr. 8, R. Ambedkar: The Bill was introduced on the 
instruction of fhe Cabinet. 


Dr, P. 8. Deshmukh: Was the motion for Gonsideration referred to the 
Cabinet for approval and has it been so approved by all the members of the 
Cabinet? 

The Honourable Dr. 8, R. Ambedkar: lt has the approval of the Gov- 

emment, Once the introdyction of ihe Bilt bas been eanetioned by the 
Cabinet no further reference to the Cabinet hss become necessary. 
. Dr. P. S. Deshmukh: Am I to understand then that since the time the 
Bil was’ introduced no further reference of any sort has been made to the 
Cabinet collectively 2 

The Bonourable Dr. B. &. Ambedkar: Oh yes: it has been. 

Dr. P. 8. Deshmukh: At what time and with what result? 


Babu Ramnarayan Singh: What is the authority under which the Honour- 
able Minister has brought about the changes in the Bill? 

The Honourable Dr. 8, R. Ambedkar: I] have not brought about any 
changes in the Bill. The changes bave been made hy the Select Committee, 
aa J. have pointed out.

DEBATES 
{PART T]—Pnocexpines OTHER THAN QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS) 
कै Monday, 28th February, 949 


Tho Assembly met in the Assembly Chamber of the Council House.at a

QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS 
(See Part ?) 


2-5 P.M. 
STATEMENT RE LOCOMOTIVES IMPORTED FROM U. 8. A. AND 
CANADA 
Tha Honourable Shri N. Gopalaswemi Ayyangar (Minister of Railways and 
Transport}: During the discussions on the Railwuy Hudget, the Honourable 
Shri Mahuvir ‘I'yagi expressed misgivings rega@Ming the order recently placed 
by the Railway Board for 800 locomotives in the U. 8.,A. and Canada; certain 
criticisms lave also appeared in the press. In my eperzh at the close of the 
debutcs on the 23rd instant I commenced to reply to these but was unable to 
contplete it for lack of time. At the request uf the boneurable members pre- 
sont aud with the concurrence of the MHonourxbio the Deputy Spenker, I 
‘undertook to make a statement to the House giving the real facts, and with 
your perinissiou, Sir, I now make this statemit in compliance with thet 
तक dertaking. 
2. Tha criticisms levelled against these looomotives are broadly— 
(]) As regards design— 
(a) that they are too heavy for Indian track; 
(t) that they are not intended to run on track sapported by woodea 
sleepers; 
(c) that they are of a type suitable only to American conditions, and wif 
rot give satisfactory performance in India; 
(9) that they are designed to draw heavier loads which will require 
considerable alteration in the exieting yerds; and * 
(8) that they are provided with bar frames wiiol: cannot be repaired in 
cur Workshops. 


(3) In respect of placing the orders— 
{a) that no effective tests of this type were conducted; 
(०) that the number ordered is!’ teo large for our requirements; and 
* (6) that orders were not placed on the Contineat of Europe. 
(3) In yenetal— 
(n) thet Mechanie¢al Engineers were not corsulted in drawing up {he 


design and that, when consulted later. they were of the opinion 
‘hat it did not fit in with Indian reqs irements; - 


(937) 


938 CONBTITUENT ASSBMELY OF INDIA (J-BGISLATIVE) (28TH rE. 94> 
(Shri N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar] * 


(b) that Government had to incur considerable expenditure on deputing 
experts to America to investigate this question; and 


(a) that a large number of locomotives of thia type which had arrived 
in India, are idling as being unsuitable. हि 


8. The recent orders for locomotives include 800 broad gauge 3४: type with 
one firw in the U. 8, A. and two firms in Canada; 20 metre gauge YP type 
engines with one firm in the U. 8, A.; and 50 tnetre gauge YG type with 
two fms in the U. S. A. 


4. The WP type wew evolved by the Railway 0०70 isn ¥46 as an Express 
Passenger locomotive designed to burn inferior grade coal, with 8 tractive 
effort almost exactly that of an older type with a heavier axle load. As a first 
step, orders were placed for producing 6 locomotives of this type as proto- 
types for trials in India. Owing to the inabiltty of the U.K. Manufacturers 
to produce these prototypes guickly, the order was trausfyr:cd to a firm in the 
U. 8. & The first of these locomotives arrived in India in October 947, sid 
was put irunediately, through tests whéch, besides the usual ones, includ 
boiler performance, fue] sousurnption with varying joads 49d on varying gru ef 
ents, oscillation and hunting. The locamotive wus also tested under ‘‘worn 
condition’. The results ‘were examined in the Central Standards Ofhee for 
Railways and by the Ruilway Board, aud the design was finalised in cousulta- 
tion with our Consulting Engineers, « British firm, whose expert personally 
visited India for the purpose. The WP type was also examined by the Yocoma9- 
tive Standarcis Committee, who raised no major objection. Whatevet modi- 
fications were considercd necessary as 38 result of the tests were incorporated 
in the final design before bulk orders were placed. aes 


5, The WP. locomotive hus un axle load of 8-5 tons as against the - dle 
lond of 9-8 tone fur the AC type Pacitic locomotive with the same tractive 
effort which has been in use in this country. The total static plus hammer 
blow loads under the driving wheels total 2°4 tons for thig type of engine ns 
against 34°5 tons for the XC type, which means a reduction of 5, per cent. We 
have at present in use in India for goods traffic a certain number of locomotives 
of tbe XE type with an axle load of over 22 tons. The WP design is therefore 
very mucli lighter than a number of types which we bave in the country includ- 
Ing eve: the type in use with the same tractive power. This locomotive is 
considered safe for use on 80 Ib. track, and most of cur main lines are inid 
with 90 lb. raile and some sections with even heavier rails, namely, 200 lb. 
and 5 ib. The factor ruling the weight and type of cngine that can cuu on 
any track is not the type of the sleeper on which the track-is laid but the

the crown’’. If a man hae property he ie afraid of death.

Our Hindu Code Bill provides in this respect that a woman should be

period the social position of women was generally high.

Now, I come to the great question which has agitated humanity from the

THE BUDGET FOR 049-60 


The Honourable Dr. John Mattha) (Minister of Finance): Sir, I rise to preaent 
‘the Budget for 949-50. : 


Review of Econumtc Conditions 


2. Although this is the fourth year since the cessation of hostilities, 
‘the return of normal conditions, without which it is impossible to expand 
production and develop trade, seems still as far of as ever. Over large parts 
of the world, conditions remain disturbed ard the progress of recovery from 
‘the ravages of the war is painfully slow. In Europe the impasse in Berlin, 
the civil war in Greece and the emergence of two rival camps among the 
countries that fought the war as allies ore symptomatic of the abnormal 
conditions which still prevail. The Palestine question, which is a serious 
threat to peace and stability in the Middle East, is’ still unsolved. Nearer 
‘home, the continuance of civil strife In China, the disturbances in Burma, 
which concern us deeply in view of our close economic ties with that country 
and the large interests our nationals have in it, and the unprovoked assault 
wf the Dutch on the Indonesian Republic, ail these retard the economic 
‘recovery not merely of this largely under-developed area of the globe but of 
the whole world. Prosperity, like peace, ‘is indivizible and while these 
conflicts are going on in various parts of the world involving a tragic waste of 
men and material, the common man in all countries, particularly the lees 
developed countries, continues to pay a heavy price in want and suffering. 


8. In our own country, 88 in most other countries in the post-war period, 
the major problem has been that of keeping the inflationary trends under 
control. It is not necessary for me to dilute on the causes of the emergence 
of these trends since the end of the war. While as a result of the war the 
purchasing péwer in the hands cf the community incrensed considerably, the 
available supply of commodities in the post-war perio! was not sufticient to 
meet the increasing demand for them. The shortage of essentin! commodities, 
particularly food and clothing, aggravuted the situotion owlig to the rise in the 
money income of some sections of the community who in the past never 
competed for food 8599 consumer _goods on the same scale. Difficulties in 
transport and distribution accentuated the shortages while the partition last year 
and the economic dislocation caused over considerzhle parts of the country 
hy the movement of population between the two Dominions made the position 
worse. A steady 786 in prices occurred throughout 3047 the general index 
nomber of wholean’y prices rising from 29.5 at the beginning of the year to 
834.2 at the end of December. In the first seven monthr of 948 there was 
a further steep rise in prices, the index number of wholesale prices rising tv 86 
much as 76 points to 390.. Since then the prices have slightly dropped and 
as a result of the various meaaures taken by Government. prices have remained 
more or less steady. 

4..The answer to the Problem of inflation and high prices is io increase 
the supply of commodities to meet the existing demand and until this position 
is reached to control the distribution of the available supply of the essential 
commodities. In the matter of food the supply position atill continues to be 
difficult and last year owing to the poor crops in certain parts of the country 
we hav, had to inmport 2 8 million tons from overseas at a oost of Re. 90 
core against an estimated import of 2 million tons at a cost of Rs. १0 crores. 
The position is expected to be still more difficult this year owing to the floods 
im Bibor and the United Provinces, thie damage caused by the recent cyclone in 
Bombe nad the outbreak of famine in parte of Gujerat, Saurashtra, Rejestban 
and Guteh and imports may amount to 4 million tons. As the House le eware, 
it hae already been decided to re-introduce food control and it is hoped that 
this will secure an equitable supply of food grains throughout the country at 
fair prices. In respect of other commodities the position in regard ‘o internal

production bas, | am glad to gay, been encouraging in recent months although

war years or th2 target that has been sct. Lust yeur the production of coul 
at 29.73 million tons und of steel at 84,000 tons just fell short of the production

favourable trends in production contiuue, } huve no doubt that it will be

‘5, Tbe stute of the capital murket in the year under review his been a 
matter of concern to Government. While there is obviously a large smouut 
of mouey in the country competing for the limited supply of goods, the 
investment murket hus been stagiuut and there has been little flow of money 
into either Government loans or into industrial concerns. ‘his stagnution 
is due iu lurge measure to the prevailing uncertainty in regard to matters 
affecting industrial development and prospects. My own view is that with 
the huge potential demand in this country for both consumer and capital goods, 
there is bound to be for many years a wide field for private enterprise und thut 
in this matter no one who invests money is taking o greater risk than in any 
other oouvutry in the world. = 

6. As I meutioned a while ago, the fight against iuflution has been one of 
Government's muin pre-occupations this year. As Honouruble Members are 
aware, Governmeit consulted varioug interes{s und sections of public opinion 
regnrdiug the zneasure's necessury to deul with this situation. lu a matter of 
this kind, it is obviously not possible to expect complete unanimity of opinion 
as to either the causes of iuflution or the remedies to be adopted. But it waa 
clear that immediute action should be taken to prevent, as fur as possible, 
the further creution of purchasing power in the hands of the commuuity and 
to take all steps possible to stimulate production and instil a spirit of confidence 
in industry. With this end in view, ii the tiekl of taxation adclitioual duties 
on certuin urticles of luxury such ug liquor, tobacco, motor care, silk wud 
art silk fubrics were imposed and au excise duty on super fine cloth was levied. 
Power was taken to make provisional assessments of iucome-tux on the basis 
of returns submitted by the asseasees. A system of interest bearing deposits 
for income-tux introduced in 943 was also revived und it wus decided to 
ele Hate for a further period of three years the refund of deposits of Excess 

rofits Tax except for approved purposes. A temporary limit wua also placed 
on the amount that may be distributed as dividend by public companies, by 
an Ordinance, the euactment of which into law is now under consideration by 
the House. Among measures taken to stimulate industrial production, I would 
mention the reduction in the import duty on machinery aud certain mdustrial

Balance of Payments 


7. The main features in the externul financial position of the country in the 
year uuder review have been the sharp decline in the sterling balances held by 
the Iteserve Bank of india, the growitrg difficulty in the financing of imports 
from the hard currency areas aud the emergence of Pukistan us a foreign country 
for currency purposes with the separation of its currency from thas of India. 
The sterling balances which reached the peak figure of Rs. ,733 crores at the 
end of 945-56 declined ty Rs. J2] crores to Re. ,62 crores during !946 47. 
This reduction was due mainly to the large imports of food but there were 
ulso substantial imports of other goods in satisfaction of the pent up demand 
of the war years 800 also a certain amount of repatriation of British capital. 
During 947-48 the reduction was somewhat smaller due to the restrictive 
import policy which was introduced towards the close of 947 and the balunces 
fell by only Rs. 67 crores to Rs. ,545 crores. In the first ten months of the 
current year there has been a (further) drop of Rs. 556 crores in these balances. 
‘This heavy reduction is due to several causes. The first is the puymie:nt to 
the United Kingdom Goverument in accordance with the agreement reached 
with them last July, of Re. 284 crores for the purchase of annuities for finuncing 
the payment of sterling pensions and for the acquisition of the Defence 
installutions and stores left behind in India by the United Kingdom ut the end 
of the war. The second is the payment to the State Bank of Pakistan of 
Pakistan's share of these balances fotlowing the separation of its currency from 
that of India. This payment is still continuing as the sterling and other assets 
of the Issue Department are handed over in instalments as Indiun notes are 
withdrawn from circulation in Pakistan and handed over to the Reserve Bank. 
Sterling to the extent of Rs. ,77 crores has so far been transferred to the 
Pakistan State Bank. The third factor responsible for the decline is India’s 
adverse balance of payments on current and capital account. 


8. Government's import policy is largely determined by the trend of the 
balance of payments. ‘The aim of this policy ix 80 to regulate trade thut while 
it is kept at the highest possible level consisteut with the needs and require- 
ment of the country, India should not have an overall deficit in her balance of 
payments on current account during any particular period of time of more than 
the amaunt by which it has been agreed with the United Kingdom Governmens$ 
that India’s sterting balances should be drawn upon. Unrler the present 
agreement signed inst July it has been agreed that India’s free sterling account 
which had 6 balance at the end of June, i048 of £ 80 million will be credited 
with v2 equivalent sum during the period July, 7949 to June, 95). In 
pursitance of this polioy and also with the immediate object of reducing the 
inflationary pressure ‘in the country, import controls were relaxed during the 
eaurse of the year, and I am glad to say they have resulted in substantially 


increasing the tivailable supply of goods in the countrv. 


$72 CONSTITUBNT ASSEMBLY OF INDIA (LEGISLATIVE) (9879 ras. 949 


{Dr. John Mathai] हि 

9. While our overall balance of payment position is on the whole satis- 
tactory, our balance of payments with the dollar and hard currency countries 
is causing us great concern. In the prewar years India had usually a surplus 
with the United States of America and during the war years owing to the 
drastic curtailment of imports to conserve dollars for the war effort, India 
continued to have increasing gurpluses. After the war, the trend of trade 
rapidly reverzed itself and, in common with the rest of the world, this country 
started having substantial deficits on hard currency account. The reason for 
this was that we had increasingly to turn to the hard currency countries for our 
requirements as the countries whose. economies had been disrupted by the war 
could not meet them. Tlris was particularly true of food which is today the 
largest single consumer of fureign exchange. Burma could not supply all the 
rice we needed because of the ravages of the war and its aftermath; Australia 
could not give us all the wheat we wanted because the United Kingdom had 
priority in supply. 

30. India’s dollar deficit ip the past used to be financed by the central 
reserves of the sterling area. But beginning from January, 2948 the United 
Kingdom refused to carry this responsibility any further and insisted on limiting 
the convertibility of our sterling very rigidly. The limits imposed— 
& 0 million (8 40 million) for the half year January--June, 3948 and 
£ 5 mitlion ($ 00 million) for the year July, 948—June, 049—these limits 
bear no relation whatever to our Needs. Concurrently with the imposition of 
these limits on convertibility, there came the separation of the exchange 
resources of Pakistan from Indiu which also took place in January, 948. 
‘this has handicapped us severely as many commodities which before partition 
earned hard currency, such as raw jute, raw cotton and hides and skins, were 
largely exported from ierritorics which ure now iucluded in Pakisten. In spite 
of the maximum possible limitation of imports from the hard currency areas 
and the maximum possible encouragement of exports thereto, India had-a 
deficit in her balance of payments with the hard currency countries in the 
six months April to September, 948 of $ 45 million. The deficit for the 
next three months, for which.now preliminary figures are avuiluble, is expected 
to be $ 48 million. Of these deficits, the purchase of food grains was responsible 
for $ 85 million and है 40 million respectively. These deficits which exceed by 
far the convertibility allowed to us by the United Kingdom have been met by 
loans from the International Monetarw Fund from which since March, ]9486 
we have borrowed no less than $ 92 million. 


]. I confess that this chronic dollar deficit is causing Government no little 
anxiety. Honourable Members are aware that we intend to negotiate dollar 
loans from the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development. But 
these joans will be available only for financing the purchase of equipment for 
our developmental projects and not for current expenditure. We do not favour 
the idea of borrowing on the present scale from the. International Monetary 
Fund but if the central reserves of the sterling area, tc which at one time 
we were 8 source of strength, insist on continuing to limit our claims on them 
in the same way os at present, we shall have no alternative to rontinuing to 
borrow. @ne ray of hope in the situation lies, of course, in the fact that ४6 hope, 
in future years, to import less food and to divert our food purchases to the soft 
currency areas. It is also possible that the international prices of food grains 
will not remain at the present high leveis. 

2. While we have exchange control witb all countries in the world, we 


have none, with Pakistan. Tbis bas been rendered possible by the fast that 
simultaveously with the separation of their currencies on the let July, 948 


THR BUDGET FOR 949-60 972 


India and Pakistan came to a monetary and payments agreement. The main 
terms of this agreement, which is in the first instance valid for a period of 
one year, are that each party will hold the currency of the other up to a limit 
of Rs. 5 crores, that thereafter up to a fuither limit of Re, l0 crores accounts 
will be settled in free sterling and the balance in Account No. II. The reason 
for entering into an agreement of this kind was that the Governments of both 
Dominions were anxious to ensure that the obstacle to inter-dominion trade, 
which the imposition of exchange contro] would necessarily entail, should, 
if possible, be avoided. I am glad to sav that thie agreement has worked satis- 
factorily and that currency considerations have not stood in the way of inter- 
dominion commerce.

7, The increase of Rs. 48-44 crores in civil expenditure is mainly due to 
three causes. First, the revised estimates include a new provision of Rs. 20-75 
crorms for tieeting pre-partition Vabilities for which no prov-sion was mode in 
the budget. At the time the budget was framed, auflicient data regarding the 
outstanding liahilities were not available and the arrangement with Pakistan 
for meeting and adjusting these had not also been settled. It was hoped that 
these liabilities would be met arid shared currently by the two Dominions but 
the Pakistan Government declined to meet these liabilities on the ground that 
they were initially those of India and that Pakistan would take its share only 
through the debt settlement. The Government of India do not accept this view 
as, in their op:nion, after the division of the available cash balances between 
the two Governments, the outstanding liabilities should be paid and currently 
shared by the two Governments. But in order to avoid hardship to those who 
‘had made supplies or rendered services to the undivided Government, the 
Government of India have agreed to meet the liabilities in the first instanoe. 
Certain outstanding payments re‘ating to the pre-partition period, such sa the 
Provincial share of income-tax, are also due to the Provincial Governments now 
loogted ‘in India which the Government of India propose to meet. Secondly, 
the expenditure on the relief and rehabilitation of refugees which hae always 
been difficult to estimate and for which a provision of Rs. IU-0$ crores was made 
in the budget is now expected to amount to Re. 9-45 crores. This increase is 
partly due to the carryover of certain liabilities from the previous year, mainly 
payments to Provincial Governments, which could not be made before the cluse 
of the venr: partly also to inereased expenbture on relief which has to be 
reguiated with reference to the constantly changing reqoirements of the situa- 
tion. Thirdly, the expenditure on the suhsidising of imported food grains nud 
the payment of honuses to Provincial Governments on internal procurement is 
now expected to exceed the original estimate by Re. 2-05 crores. The House 
will remember that after a review of the food position last. October it was decided 
that with effect from the-lst October 948 the Central share of the loss on the 
supply of imported food grains to deficit areas should be raised from two thirds 
to three quarters and that a similar concers:on, limited to the Centre meeting 
haf the loss. should h. extended w the Indian States. Part of the increase is 
also due to the fact that while the budget provided for subsidies up to the 
end of the enlendur scar, provision has now been rmucle for the full fmancial vear. 


I should also mention a further reason for the increase in civil expenditure. 
The House will remember that in the budget for the current year my predeces- 
sor made a lump ent o! Ks, 2.5 crores for economies likely to result from the 
implementation of the recommendations of the Economy Committee. The work 
of this Committee has taken somewhat longer than was originally expested and 
ite report on a number of Ministries is still under examination. No envings are 
therefore likely to be realised this year. For next year I am not making any 
provision at this stage as it is not possible, until firm decisions have been taken 
on their recommendations, to estimate the savings likely to be renlised. But 
I may assure the House that this does not mean that I do not expect economies 
to result from the Committee's recommendations. On the contrary. it is my 
intention to secure the utmost economy possible in public expenditure and the 
House may rest assured that the recommendations of the Committee will be 
moat carefully and eamestly considered by Government and the resulting econo- 
mies enforaed during the course of the year.

Tncome-tax Investigation Commission 


8. Before I pass on to deal with the estimates for the coming year I should 
like to mention the progress made in the work of the Income.tax Investigation 
Commission. Honourab'e Mensbers will remember that the Act constituting 
the Comm'ssion assigned two duties to it. first. to investigate and report on all 
matters relating to taxation on income, with particular reference to the extent 
to which the existing law relating to and procedure for the assessment and 
collection of such taxation is adequate to prevent evasion and secondly to in- 
vestigate specific cases referred to the Commission by the Central Government. 
Cn a study of the working of the Income-tax lnw and its administration during 
recent years, the Commission came to the conclusion thet on a long-term view 
the first task was nc less important than the second, and as work on the second 
had for various reasons necessarily to be siow, the Commission devoted ३ great 
purt ef its time till recently to the first task. This involved the examination of 
voluminous evidence tendered in reply to a comprehensive questionnaire which 
was issued, and the Commission has recently submitted a long report in which 
it has mado recommendations on many pointe of law and ot Administration 
These recommendations are now being examined with a view to the necessary 
leg’slation being introduced, and it is hoped that it will be possible to place a 
Bill before the House at ite next session. Copies of the Commission's report. 
IT may say, will be distributed to Hcnourable Members a'ong with the Budget 
papers before the House rises today, 


79. As regards the investigation of specific cases referred to it by Govern. 
Tent, the Commission has completed a few cases. Greater progress was not 
possible for a variety of reasons. Firstly. it was only in April 948 that the 
necessary staff fer investigation work could be placed at the disposal of the 

Commission. This was because it was not possible for the Income-tax Depart 
rent to spare their more experienced officers for this work before April, 048, in 
view of the shortage: of officers in the Department and the large amount of 
errears that had ६0 be collected. However, this difficulty has new been over- 
come to some extent, and the officers auther’sed by the Commission are work- 
ing in different cities carrying on investigation under instructions ard directions 
given by the Commission. It is the materia! gathered by them that will form 
the basis of the Commission’s further work. 


Another factor which has delayed the disposal of specific cases taken up for 
enquiry ts that it: has been found that & good deal of general enquiry and colles- 
tion of facts and figures is a necessary preliminary to the investigation of the 
specific cases. These general encuiries and the collation of the materials col- 
‘lected have taken a consderable time, but.the work is proceeding apace. Once 
this is completed, it may be hoped that the disposal of specific cases will be 
expedited. 


In this connection I may add that the possibility of disposing of the referred 
cases by agreed settlement is being explored, and a Bill will shortiy be placed 
‘before the House for vesting the necessary powers for making such settlements 
in the hands of the Commission.

28. Defence Services.—Following the customary procedure I shall frst deal 
with the Defence. estiniates. The Defence Budget for the coming year shows 
an increase of Rs. .94 crores over the revised estimaie for the current year; 
the excess in which I iargely ascribed to the operations in Kashmir. The House 
tyey well ask why with the cease fire in lXashmir there is no decrease in the- 
Defence Budget. The main-reason for this is that the reduction in the strength 
of the armed forces cannot: be made over-night and the process of demobilisa- 
tion bas necessarily to be spread over a period similar’ to the recruitment and 
training of the forces. The currert year's revised estimates retlect the ex- 
penditure on the gradnal building up of ovr strength while the budget for next 
Yeor would retlect the gradnal reduction in that strength. Since both the 
Processes take time, the average strength of the troops during the budget year 
is unlikely to differ materially from that in the current year. In framing the 
budget for next year we have taken into account the improvement in the Kash- 
mir situation following the cease fire and our hope that this cease fire will 
eventually !ead to a peaceful solution of the problem. The House will natural- 
ly not expect me to go into greater detail in this matter hut I must utter a 
warning that if for any unforeseen reason these hopes on which we have hased 
our estimates are not realised, it may be necessary to exceed the provision 
now proposed. It bas also to be remembered that the renent grant of an ad hee

em=msts allowance to omployees of the Central Government hes added

As the House is uware, our Navy till recently consisted of only a few sloops and.

ming importance of the fleet air arm and the House will be glad to know tbat. 
a beginning has already been.made in this respect and that the budget includes.

and effective force. * 


24. Before IJ pass on to civil estimates I would like to refer to a change ia 
the preparation of the Defence Demands for next year. The provision for eup- 
plies and stores which was previously pooled together and included under one 
Demand for all the three Services, except for cerlain Naval and Air Force 
stores, has now been eplit among the thren Services---Army, Navy and Air

27. Honourable Members are aware that in recent months a large number of

sent the transactions of these States have been kept separate in a deposit

be hoped, the integration is completed in the course of the coming year, these

Ways and Means 


82, I now turn to a brief consideration of the ways and meana_ position. 
érvu. rhe current yeur’s budget provided for a total borrowing of Rs. 50 
erores from the market und to a net receipt of Re. 32:25 crores from 

small savings. For a variety of reasons to which I have referred in another part 
‘of ney speech ¢he zilt edged market remained inactive throughout the year 
with very little investment demand. It was not therefore possible to borrow 
on the scale originally contemplated. For next year I have made a modest 


980 CONST(TUBNT ASSEMBLY OF INDIA (LEGISLATIVE) [9878 rep. 949: 


(Dr. John Motthai} 


provision of Rs. 85 crores for murket loans. But if. 88 I hope, conditions 
improve the scale of borrowing will be raised. Next year Government heave 
the option, of repaying the 3 per cent. loan १949-52 with an outstandmg balances. 
of Rs. 67 crores. I have assumed that (४४8 amount will be repaid and in my 
estimate of borrowing next year I have taken this return of money to the market 
algo ints» account. in the present inflationary cenditions. it is of the outmost 
importance for the economy of the country that the community should save 
as much as: possible and lend its savings to the State. I trust that the recent 
measures taken by Government to meet the inflationary situation would help in 
restoring public confidence and that this time next year I shall be in a posi- 
tion to give a more heartening picture of public co-operation in the Government’s 
borrowing operations. As regards smal] savings, there has been in recent 
months some improvement ia the net receipts from Savings Banks and in the 
sale of National Savinge Certificates. The House will remember that last 
April Government issued two new series of certificates with a currency of five 
@nd seven yeara to cater to the smna‘ler investor and algo raised the maximum 
limit of investanent in both Postal Stvings Bank and National Savings Certi-_ 
Goates. It is too early to assess the result of these measures but Government 
are doing all in their power, in co-operation with the Provincial Governments, 
to stimulate and sustain the smull savings movement. It is hoped that the 
budget anticipations in regard to receints from this sourme will be exceeded 
this year hy Re.l°6 crores. For next year J have allowed for some further im- 
provement and taken credit for a not receipt of Rs. 37-56 crores. 


33. I now pass on to my proposals for dealing with the deficit of Rs. 4-79’ 
crores anticipated in the coming year. ‘ 


34, The prospective deficit.is substantial and I am sure that the House will. 
agree with me that in the present inflationary conditions it should not be left 
uncovered. 


, 35. The probiem before me is aot merely that of raising the additional 
revenue to cover this deficit. I have also to consider the adjustments in taxation 
necessary in the light of the experience of the working of the tax system in 
the perod since the House passed the budget for the current year. Fiscal 
policy is not an end in itself but has to subserve the ends of national policy and 
in @ trans‘t:onal period Jike this it is essential to keep the working of the 
taxation system under constant review and readjust it in the light of changing 
circumstances. 


Apart from inflation, to which I have referred in detail eleewhere, the mort 
disconcert.ng element in the economic life of the country today ia 6 deep, 
underlying fear of the future, of which the stagnation in the capital market 
is an index. In niy view one of the most urgent taaks before a Finance Minie- 
ter today is to concert measures designed to remove this fear and to secure 
8 revival of confidence. It is clear from recent experience that the formation 
of capital in this country has been seriously affected, with the result that 
investments in both Government loans and industry have been falling off. 
Unless this stagnation is checked and conditions are created in wh'ch the 
incentive to save and to invest is revived, the industrial expansion of the 
country and the execution of the plans for raising the living atendards of the 
peaple are bound to be delayed. In formulating the proposals which I shall 
place before the House, I have kept this requirement prominently in mind. 


Reliefs in Taxation 


me 36. I shall firet deal with the various measures of relief which I propose 
Give. 


THR BUDGET For 949-80 982 


In the field of direct taxation, my 7760 proposal is to abolish the Cupital 
‘Gains Tax. At the time this tax was introduced it was expeoted to wield a large 
revenue, but it synchronised with a period of falling values and the yield 
from this tax has, in consequence, been emall. Its psychological effect on 
investment has, however, been markedly adverse and it has had the effect of 
humpering the free movement of stocks and shares, without which it is 
.hard!y possible to maintain a high level of industrial development. In pre- 
-gent crcumstances, I consider the retention of this tax ill-advised. The loss 
-of revenue is estimated at Re. | crore. 


My second proposal re.ates to income-tax. Here I propose to give some 
-Telief to income-tax payers in the lowest and medium income groups. The 
tax on incomes upto Res. 0,000 a year will be reduced by a quarter of an 
‘anne fron one anne to nine pies in the first slab and from two annas to one 
‘anne nine pies in the secund slab. ‘This class has been severely hit by the rise 
in prices and a certain degree of ‘relief in their case is justified. The loss ia 
estimated at Rs. 3 crores. 

My third proposal relates to super-tax. Here I propose two reliefs designed 
to meet the criticism that the existing level of taxation leaves. little incentive 
‘for saving and investment and that it is illogical to ignore the differentiation 
between earned and unearned income above Re. व3 lakhs. In respect of earned 
income I propose a reduction of an anne and a half in the rates charged on 
incomes above Rs. § lakhs, leaving the maximum rate of tax for income- 
tax and super-tax together at l4 annas. For unearned income, I propose a 
reduction of 6 pies in the maximum fate of super-tax. The cost of the two 
concessions will be Rs. 2° crorea, 

Of the total loss of Rs. 6:] crores involved in these ooncessions, Rs. 8 
crores will fall on the Provinces by reducing the divisible pool of income-tax 
and the balance on the Centre. 


87. Before I leave the subject of direct taxation I would like to mention. 
two changes which I propose to make. The House will remember that in the 
budget for the current year my predecessor gave @ concession to companies 
with an income of Rs, 28,000 and below by reducing their income-tax to half 
the usual rates. This concession was meant to encourage the growth of 
smiller companies but the reduction, which was allowed in income-taz, has 
given r.se to considerable administrative difficulties, wholly out of proportion 
to the amount involved or the benefit accruing to the companies. I have 
carefully reviewed the position and come to the conclusion that while the 
concess:on should be continued it should take the form of a rebate of half ths 
Corporation Tax. and should be lim‘ted further to public-controlled small com- 
panies which are not branches or subsidiaries of bigger companies. The result 
of this change will be that the entire cost of the concession will fall upon the 
Centre, and the Prov:nces will not have to share it. The amount involved is 
tkely to be small and I have therefore made no specific provision on this 
account in the estimates for next year. The second change relates to the 
taxation of incomes of privately-controlled companies which do not. declare 
their d:vidends in India, It may be recalled that there was a serious anomaly 
in the a§ministration of inoome-tax law relating to the recovery of sper-tax 
from shareholders, in respect of the dividends paid out of Indian profits, by 
companies incorporated outside this country. It was difficult to obtain from 
these companies information concerning the names of their shareholders and 
the amouns of dividends paid out of Indian profits add coatequently there was 
a considerable Joss of revenue. The problem of plugging this leakage was conal- 


982 CONSTITUENT ASSEMBLY OF INDIA (LEGISLATIVE) [987४ एड8, 949: 
[Dr. John Matthai] 

dered last year and my predecessor introduced a scheme whereby an extra ‘tux 
of one anna was imposed on all such companies with a view partally to recoup: 
ing this loss. Ae part of the scheme, an amendment was made to the Income- 
tux Act so as to confer persounl immunity from further taxation upon the 
share-ho'ders of such companies. The amendnient. however, had the effect of 
conierring mmunity from super-tax not mer¢ly upon the dividends actually 
received, but also upon the dividends which under the operation of section 
233-A of the Income-tax Act could be deemed to have been received 
from _privately-controlled companies. Therefore, if the matter had 
been left there, the profits of these companies would have escaped with an 
uverall impost much lighter than that to which they were subject under the pre- 
vous iaw. Ths point was met by applying to this category of companies the 
rates of income-tax and super-tax prescribed for individuals or aasociations and. 
the definition of ‘‘company’’ was altered to permit of this being done by exe- 
cutive action. The arrmgement, however, lias not been satisfactory, uid after a 
cereful review of thé matter I have decided that instead of uttempting to tax 
ench such privately-controlled company as an. individual, the principle of 
spplying an average rate should be adopted. I accordingly propose that all 
corporntions, whether Ind.an or non-Indian, should continue to be treated as 
companies but a further super-tax of one anna should be paid by those pr-vate- 
ly-controlled companies that do not distribute their profits in India. J propose 
to upply this method commencing with the current year. It will not involve 
any change in the revenue estimates. 


38. There is one aspect of the complaint about the high taxation in relation 
to industry to which I would like to make a passing reference. Owing to the 
- steep risé in the level of prices of raw materials, wages and working costs, 
lurge amounts of working capital are needed (० maintain production. Re- 
placement costs are also higher, and there have been complaints that the 
calculation of depreciation allowance for purposes of taxatican on the original cost 
of the usset involves great hardship. It has been suggested that industrics 
should be allowed to revalue their existing fixed assets-at the present day prices 
so that future depreciat.on allowance may be given on the bass of the revalua- 
tion. The Government of India considered this problem in all its aspecte last 
October and came to the conclusion that while the difficulty complained of was 
real the solution proposed was not practicable. It would give no assistance to 
those who have immediately to replace their worn-out assete and there was no 
poit in giving a concession to others who, at some future date, may not be 
required to pay the high prices now prevailing. It was, however, realised that 
some relief should be given to those who were prepared to renew and re-equip 
their capital assets immediately, in spite of the prevailing high coate. It was 
decided that for all new plant and machinery installed during the five years 
f-oin the ist April 948, deprec‘at:‘on allowance at double the ordinary rate wil} 
be allowed. It has also been decided that if by Ist April 953 there is a drop in 
the general level of prices, the difference between the writen down value of the 
assets and the corresponding value at the reduced price will be allowed as an 
additional depreciation allowance. For existing plant and mechienry, it has 
been decided to grant extra depreciation allowance for increased wear and tear 
if triple shifts are worked. I trust that these concessions will go a long way 
in meeting the complaints of industry in this matter. In addition, the conces- 
8:00 given last year of a reduced rate of income-tax to companies which do not 
ciatr,bute a part of their profits as dividends, and the recent |'mitation, for a 
temporary period, of the amount that may be distributed as dividends will a'so 
eiiable industrial concerns to accumulate reserves for meeting the increased 


ont of replacement.

42. I now come to the problem of covering the remaining deficit of Ra.

45. The excise duty on cloth has a long and bitter history behind’ it and 
I must explain to the House my reasons for introducing it. In the first place, 
the circumstances in which we are levying the duty today are different from 
those in which it was levied by ७ foreign Government in the _ interests 
-of a foreign industry. In the second place, it is necessary to replace the 
heavy loss in revenue resulting from the abolition of the salt duty by develop. 
ing some other equally stable source of revenue. Cloth, with a large internal 
produotion and as an article of universal consumption, offers itself as the rnost 
-obvious choice for a tax on consumption. Thirdly, in the present conditions,

of rationalising the duty on matches, whioh I have had under consideration for 
some time The {deal arrengemmt will be to bave one standard aise of 
matches but owing to praotioal difSculties in production it ie not possible to 
achieve this. I have therefore decided that two sizes, namely, 40s and 60a 
should continue in production and I have made 6096 slight readjustment in 
fhe rate of duty. Factories whose annual output is leas than 5 lakhe gross 
boxes will now benefit by the levy of a somewhat lower rate of duty. Neoces- 
sary amendmente to the tariff wii] be made through the Finance Bill bug thfs 
will not involve any change in the revenue estimates. With the provision of 
hhe two sizes I hope that tte retail prices will not exceed 6 pies and 9 pies 
per box and that the consumer will get the benefit of the partial standardisation 
without any loss of revenne (0 the exchequer. 


Net result of propoxala 


47, I may now summarise the net effect of all the proposala that I bave 
placed before ९७० House. The varlous measures of relief which I have aug- 
gested in direct taxes will result in a lose of revenue of Ra, 8:l crores; the 
abolition of the export duty on oil seeds and vegetable oils will involve a loss 
of Res. 4.8 crores; and the rebate of duty on aviation spirit and industrial raw 
materisis will cost a further sum of Rs. 75 lakhs. As I have already stated, 
this will bring the ६088) loss of revenue #0 Re. &85 orores, raising the prospec- 
tive deficit from Re. 4-79 crores to Rs. 9074 crores. The increases in ous- 
tons dutics and, the new export duty on cigarettes and cigars will yield Rs. 
628 crores the net increase in Postal rates Ra. 2:84 crores, and the increases 
in Central Excise Duties Rs. :52 orores. The final effeof of Hhese proposals 
ia to convert the prospective deficit into a emall surplus of Ra. 45 lakhs. 


Conclusion 
48. Sir. EF have come to the end of my story. It is not pleasant for a 


Finance Minister to appear before the House with a record of deficits and 
proposals for additional taxation but a Finance Minister is as much the orea- 
Sure of circumstances as any one else. Part of the heavy expenditure whiokf 
Goveriment have now to meet is 00७ to ertraordinary circumstances 
and but for these special demands, the budget would have shown a substantial 
surplus. As I have mentioned earlier, the present economic conditions in 
the country make it an imperative necessity to balance the budget. In Jaying 
freeh burdens of taxation the House will accept my assurance that I have 
done my best to secure that they are equitable and that no section of the 
community is made to pay more than ite fair share.

086 CUNSTITUENT ASSEMBY. OF INDIA (LE@ISL ATIVE) ' 8458 B, cr 


(Dr. John Matthai} 


49. On a survey of world conditions today’, I feel that we have good 
Teason for taking 8 hopeful view of our financial position. We are not alone 
in having to fight scarcities and inflation. These problems confront most 
countries in the post-war world. We can, however, take comfort from the 
fact that, unlike some other countries, our financial position is inttinsically 
sound. We have only a moderate public debt in relation to our national 
income and we have considerahle external reserves with practically no exter- 
ual debt. We have weathered the storm and stress of the partition and ite 
terrible aftermath. In spite of the heavy demands on our resources for the 
relief avd rehabilitation of refugees, the import of food on an unprecedented 
scale frors overseas and thi: detence of Kashmir syaiust aygressiou, we aro 
fn 8 position to balance our budget, without saorificing any of our essential 
schemes of developuieut. We have made some headway in the fight, against 
6-80 pM. inflation. The curve of production is slowly rising and we hyve plans 

*-"" in hand for increasing the food production of the country. I do not 
wish in any way to ininimise our difficulties or suggest that we can take a 
‘oomplacent view of the situation. A balanced national budget may, and 
offen does, cover 8 multitude of ill-balanced family budgeta. In this respeot, 
we have still a formidable task ahead of us, the task of fighting want, siok- 
ness and poverty and raising the living standards of the millions to whom the 
emancipation of the country will be a mockery unless it is translated in terms 
of opportunities for a fuller, freer and better life. This task is nob beyond our 
Tesources. but it requires the co-operation of all olasaes and secticns of the 
community in & spirit of partnership in a high adventure. I have no doubt 
that this co-operation will be forthcoming and I pray that my stewardship of 
the finances of the country may contribute in some degree to the acoomplisb- 


ment of this task.
